Um, I guess the main thing is my face. Specifically the left side—it looks uneven and kind of puffy to me, especially on video calls. I do computer support, so I’m on calls a lot, and I end up staring at my own little camera box instead of listening sometimes.

And then there are salsa photos— I dance salsa, and people post pictures, and I’ll zoom in and compare sides of my face. Everyone keeps saying they don’t see what I’m talking about, which almost makes it worse because then I feel like I’m either crazy or they’re just being nice. It’s starting to affect work and social stuff, so… yeah, that’s why I came in.

Yeah… I mean, yes. It’s not just annoying, it’s kind of taking over more than I want to admit.

At work I’ll be helping someone with their computer and then suddenly I’m thinking about how my face looked in the call window earlier, like the left side being puffy or uneven.\n\nAnd with salsa, even while I’m dancing I’ll think, “Oh no, if someone takes a photo from this angle it’s going to show.” Which is ridiculous because I’m supposed to be enjoying the music—there’s this one club where the lighting is awful, actually—but yeah, the main thing is I can’t stop focusing on it, even though people keep telling me they don’t see anything. It’s starting to really get in the way of work and being social.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are significantly affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… humiliating.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events if I knew people were going to be taking photos. Which is sad because I used to really look forward to those. I still love dancing—there’s this one song they play that always gets everyone up—but then I start thinking about pictures and angles and I just don’t go. So yeah, it’s definitely affecting my happiness.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social interactions or activities because you were worried about how others would perceive your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, especially if I know there might be photos. Salsa nights are the big one, because people are always taking pictures or posting little clips, and then I end up thinking about my face instead of just dancing.\n\nI’ve skipped some community events too for the same reason. It’s embarrassing because sometimes I’ll make up an excuse, like I’m tired or I have errands, but really it’s because I’m worried about how my face is going to look to people.\n\nAnd even at work, it’s affected me—like I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on camera, and I’ve missed ticket deadlines after getting stuck on it. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… awful. So yes, I guess I have been avoiding things.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your concern always about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy, or do you sometimes worry about other aspects of your appearance as well?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Salsa nights are the big one, because people are always taking pictures or posting little clips, and then I end up thinking about my face instead of just dancing.\n\nI’ve skipped some community events too for the same reason. It’s embarrassing because sometimes I’ll make up an excuse, like I’m tired or I have errands, but really it’s because I’m worried about how my face is going to look to people.\n\nAnd even at work, it’s affected me—like I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on camera, and I’ve missed ticket deadlines after getting stuck on it. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… awful. So yes, I guess I have been avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern always about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy, or do you sometimes worry about other aspects of your appearance as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that, yeah—the left side of my face looking uneven or puffy. That’s the thing I get stuck on, especially on video calls or if someone takes salsa photos.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll start thinking the whole photo looks bad, or the lighting makes me look strange—there’s this one place we dance where the lights are just terrible—but it always kind of comes back to that left side of my face. People keep telling me they don’t see it, which almost makes me feel more weird, because I still can’t stop noticing it. It’s really why I came in, honestly, because it’s starting to affect work and going out.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your concern about your face always focused on the left side being uneven or puffy, or does it sometimes shift to other parts of your face?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / computer_support_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can't stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks on video calls and in salsa photos, even though everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything, and it's starting to mess with my work and social life.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mostly the left side, honestly. Like the unevenness or puffiness there is what I keep coming back to, especially on video calls or in salsa photos.

Sometimes I’ll start fussing over the whole picture, like the angle or the lighting—there was this one photo from dancing where everyone else looked normal and I just kept zooming in, which is ridiculous because I know people were probably looking at the dancing, not my face. But even then, it kind of circles back to that left side.

And people keep saying they don’t see anything, which should make me feel better, but it doesn’t really. That’s part of why I came in… I can’t seem to stop focusing on it, and it’s starting to mess with work and social stuff.

Not really… I mean, maybe if I’m completely distracted for a bit, like if I’m troubleshooting something at work and someone’s printer is doing that thing where it says it’s offline even though it’s clearly not—sorry, that’s not really the point.

It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff because I’m so focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking at it, like when you're working or dancing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. I wish I could say yes, but… not really, not in the situations where it matters. Like if I’m just at home doing laundry or making coffee, maybe it’s in the background more. But at work, especially with video calls, it can get really out of hand.

I’ve joined calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the camera preview—adjusting the angle, checking the lighting, turning it off and on. And then I’m late, and it looks unprofessional. I’ve also missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck on it instead of just finishing the support issue, which is embarrassing because normally I’m pretty reliable. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, not the face thing exactly, but the lateness and missed deadlines.

And socially… salsa used to be such a fun thing for me. I mean, I still love the music and the community, and there’s this one song they always play that gets everyone on the floor—but if I know people are going to be taking photos, I’ll sometimes just not go. Same with community events. I’ll think, “Okay, there are going to be pictures posted later,” and then I talk myself out of it. So… no, I wouldn’t say it’s completely under control.

It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff because I’m so focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking at it, like when you're working or dancing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. Like even when I’m not looking at myself, it’s still kind of running in the background. At work I’ll be helping someone with their computer and then suddenly I’m thinking about how my face looked in the call window earlier, like the left side being puffy or uneven.\n\nAnd with salsa, even while I’m dancing I’ll think, “Oh no, if someone takes a photo from this angle it’s going to show.” Which is ridiculous because I’m supposed to be enjoying the music—there’s this one club where the lighting is awful, actually—but yeah, the main thing is I can’t stop focusing on it, even though people keep telling me they don’t see anything. It’s starting to really get in the way of work and being social.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are significantly affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes. It’s not just annoying, it’s kind of taking over more than I want to admit.\n\nLike at work, I’ve missed ticket deadlines because I’ll get stuck before or after a video call thinking about how my face looks, and I’ve even joined calls late because I couldn’t get myself together with the camera. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… humiliating.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events if I knew people were going to be taking photos. Which is sad because I used to really look forward to those. I still love dancing—there’s this one song they play that always gets everyone up—but then I start thinking about pictures and angles and I just don’t go. So yeah, it’s definitely affecting my happiness.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social interactions or activities because you were worried about how others would perceive your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, especially if I know there might be photos. Salsa nights are the big one, because people are always taking pictures or posting little clips, and then I end up thinking about my face instead of just dancing.\n\nI’ve skipped some community events too for the same reason. It’s embarrassing because sometimes I’ll make up an excuse, like I’m tired or I have errands, but really it’s because I’m worried about how my face is going to look to people.\n\nAnd even at work, it’s affected me—like I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on camera, and I’ve missed ticket deadlines after getting stuck on it. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… awful. So yes, I guess I have been avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern always about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy, or do you sometimes worry about other aspects of your appearance as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that, yeah—the left side of my face looking uneven or puffy. That’s the thing I get stuck on, especially on video calls or if someone takes salsa photos.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll start thinking the whole photo looks bad, or the lighting makes me look strange—there’s this one place we dance where the lights are just terrible—but it always kind of comes back to that left side of my face. People keep telling me they don’t see it, which almost makes me feel more weird, because I still can’t stop noticing it. It’s really why I came in, honestly, because it’s starting to affect work and going out.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face always focused on the left side being uneven or puffy, or does it sometimes shift to other parts of your face?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the left side, honestly. Like the unevenness or puffiness there is what I keep coming back to, especially on video calls or in salsa photos.\n\nSometimes I’ll start fussing over the whole picture, like the angle or the lighting—there was this one photo from dancing where everyone else looked normal and I just kept zooming in, which is ridiculous because I know people were probably looking at the dancing, not my face. But even then, it kind of circles back to that left side.\n\nAnd people keep saying they don’t see anything, which should make me feel better, but it doesn’t really. That’s part of why I came in… I can’t seem to stop focusing on it, and it’s starting to mess with work and social stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't think about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe if I’m completely distracted for a bit, like if I’m troubleshooting something at work and someone’s printer is doing that thing where it says it’s offline even though it’s clearly not—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut no, it keeps coming back. That’s kind of why I came in. I can’t stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks, especially on video calls or in salsa photos. And everyone keeps telling me they don’t see anything, but that doesn’t really make it go away. It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff, so… yeah, I don’t feel like I get much of a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel like your concerns about your face are completely under control and don't interfere with what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, but… not really, not in the situations where it matters. Like if I’m just at home doing laundry or making coffee, maybe it’s in the background more. But at work, especially with video calls, it can get really out of hand.\n\nI’ve joined calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the camera preview—adjusting the angle, checking the lighting, turning it off and on. I’ve also missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck on it instead of just finishing the support issue, which is embarrassing because normally I’m pretty reliable. Yeah… I think it is, indirectly at least. Like, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events when I knew people were going to be taking photos, and that means I’m not seeing people I normally would.

It’s not like my friends have done anything wrong. They’ll post pictures and everyone looks happy, and then I’ll get stuck thinking about how I’d look in them. There’s this whole group chat after salsa sometimes where people send photos, and I just… I don’t know, I avoid it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess the main thing is my face. Specifically the left side—it looks uneven and kind of puffy to me, especially on video calls. I do computer support, so I’m on calls a lot, and I end up staring at my own little camera box instead of listening sometimes.\n\nAnd then there are salsa photos— I dance salsa, and people post pictures, and I’ll zoom in and compare sides of my face. Everyone keeps saying they don’t see what I’m talking about, which almost makes it worse because then I feel like I’m either crazy or they’re just being nice. It’s starting to affect work and social stuff, so… yeah, that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face being uneven and puffy only about how it looks, or are you also experiencing any other symptoms like pain or discomfort?\n\nPatient: It’s really about how it looks, I think. Like, I don’t have some big pain thing I came in for… it’s more that I can’t stop noticing the left side of my face looking uneven or puffy, especially on video calls.\n\nAnd then with salsa pictures—sorry, I know I keep coming back to that—but people post them and I end up zooming in, comparing my face, and everyone says they don’t see it. But I do, and then I can’t let it go. It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff because I’m so focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking at it, like when you're working or dancing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. Like even when I’m not looking at myself, it’s still kind of running in the background. At work I’ll be helping someone with their computer and then suddenly I’m thinking about how my face looked in the call window earlier, like the left side being puffy or uneven.\n\nAnd with salsa, even while I’m dancing I’ll think, “Oh no, if someone takes a photo from this angle it’s going to show.” Which is ridiculous because I’m supposed to be enjoying the music—there’s this one club where the lighting is awful, actually—but yeah, the main thing is I can’t stop focusing on it, even though people keep telling me they don’t see anything. It’s starting to really get in the way of work and being social.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are significantly affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes. It’s not just annoying, it’s kind of taking over more than I want to admit.\n\nLike at work, I’ve missed ticket deadlines because I’ll get stuck before or after a video call thinking about how my face looks, and I’ve even joined calls late because I couldn’t get myself together with the camera. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… humiliating.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events if I knew people were going to be taking photos. Which is sad because I used to really look forward to those. I still love dancing—there’s this one song they play that always gets everyone up—but then I start thinking about pictures and angles and I just don’t go. So yeah, it’s definitely affecting my happiness.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social interactions or activities because you were worried about how others would perceive your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, especially if I know there might be photos. Salsa nights are the big one, because people are always taking pictures or posting little clips, and then I end up thinking about my face instead of just dancing.\n\nI’ve skipped some community events too for the same reason. It’s embarrassing because sometimes I’ll make up an excuse, like I’m tired or I have errands, but really it’s because I’m worried about how my face is going to look to people.\n\nAnd even at work, it’s affected me—like I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on camera, and I’ve missed ticket deadlines after getting stuck on it. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… awful. So yes, I guess I have been avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern always about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy, or do you sometimes worry about other aspects of your appearance as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that, yeah—the left side of my face looking uneven or puffy. That’s the thing I get stuck on, especially on video calls or if someone takes salsa photos.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll start thinking the whole photo looks bad, or the lighting makes me look strange—there’s this one place we dance where the lights are just terrible—but it always kind of comes back to that left side of my face. People keep telling me they don’t see it, which almost makes me feel more weird, because I still can’t stop noticing it. It’s really why I came in, honestly, because it’s starting to affect work and going out.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face always focused on the left side being uneven or puffy, or does it sometimes shift to other parts of your face?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the left side, honestly. Like the unevenness or puffiness there is what I keep coming back to, especially on video calls or in salsa photos.\n\nSometimes I’ll start fussing over the whole picture, like the angle or the lighting—there was this one photo from dancing where everyone else looked normal and I just kept zooming in, which is ridiculous because I know people were probably looking at the dancing, not my face. But even then, it kind of circles back to that left side.\n\nAnd people keep saying they don’t see anything, which should make me feel better, but it doesn’t really. That’s part of why I came in… I can’t seem to stop focusing on it, and it’s starting to mess with work and social stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't think about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe if I’m completely distracted for a bit, like if I’m troubleshooting something at work and someone’s printer is doing that thing where it says it’s offline even though it’s clearly not—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut no, it keeps coming back. That’s kind of why I came in. I can’t stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks, especially on video calls or in salsa photos. And everyone keeps telling me they don’t see anything, but that doesn’t really make it go away. It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff, so… yeah, I don’t feel like I get much of a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel like your concerns about your face are completely under control and don't interfere with what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, but… not really, not in the situations where it matters. Like if I’m just at home doing laundry or making coffee, maybe it’s in the background more. But at work, especially with video calls, it can get really out of hand.\n\nI’ve joined calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the camera preview—adjusting the angle, checking the lighting, turning it off and on. And then I’m late, and it looks unprofessional. I’ve also missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck on it instead of just finishing the support issue, which is embarrassing because normally I’m pretty reliable. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, not the face thing exactly, but the lateness and missed deadlines.\n\nAnd socially… salsa used to be such a fun thing for me. I mean, I still love the music and the community, and there’s this one song they always play that gets everyone on the floor—but if I know people are going to be taking photos, I’ll sometimes just not go. Same with community events. I’ll think, “Okay, there are going to be pictures posted later,” and then I talk myself out of it. So… no, I wouldn’t say it’s completely under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, indirectly at least. Like, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events when I knew people were going to be taking photos, and that means I’m not seeing people I normally would.\n\nIt’s not like my friends have done anything wrong. They’ll post pictures and everyone looks happy, and then I’ll get stuck thinking about how I’d look in them. There’s this whole group chat after salsa sometimes where people send photos, and I just… I don’t know, I avoid it.\n\nAnd at work it’s been affecting things too—video calls, ticket deadlines. I’ve joined calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked, and I’ve missed deadlines because I got caught up in it. My supervisor brought that up in a performance review, which was awful. So I guess with friends and family it’s more that I pull back, and with work it’s more obvious.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it does. I mean, salsa used to be this thing where I could just go and have fun, and not think so much—well, mostly. There’s always someone taking pictures now, though. Like everyone has their phone out, and then the photos end up in a group chat or on social media, and I start thinking about how my face is going to look from the left side.

So I’ve skipped salsa nights because of that. And community events too, if I know there’ll probably be photos. Which is kind of sad, because those are people I actually like being around. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or I have errands, but really it’s because I don’t want to see myself in pictures later.

And it’s not just friends… at work I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the preview screen, and I’ve missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck on it. My supervisor brought it up in a performance review, which was really embarrassing. So, yeah, it’s definitely getting in the way of enjoying things and just being present with people.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess the main thing is my face. Specifically the left side—it looks uneven and kind of puffy to me, especially on video calls. I do computer support, so I’m on calls a lot, and I end up staring at my own little camera box instead of listening sometimes.\n\nAnd then there are salsa photos— I dance salsa, and people post pictures, and I’ll zoom in and compare sides of my face. Everyone keeps saying they don’t see what I’m talking about, which almost makes it worse because then I feel like I’m either crazy or they’re just being nice. It’s starting to affect work and social stuff, so… yeah, that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face being uneven and puffy only about how it looks, or are you also experiencing any other symptoms like pain or discomfort?\n\nPatient: It’s really about how it looks, I think. Like, I don’t have some big pain thing I came in for… it’s more that I can’t stop noticing the left side of my face looking uneven or puffy, especially on video calls.\n\nAnd then with salsa pictures—sorry, I know I keep coming back to that—but people post them and I end up zooming in, comparing my face, and everyone says they don’t see it. But I do, and then I can’t let it go. It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff because I’m so focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking at it, like when you're working or dancing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. Like even when I’m not looking at myself, it’s still kind of running in the background. At work I’ll be helping someone with their computer and then suddenly I’m thinking about how my face looked in the call window earlier, like the left side being puffy or uneven.\n\nAnd with salsa, even while I’m dancing I’ll think, “Oh no, if someone takes a photo from this angle it’s going to show.” Which is ridiculous because I’m supposed to be enjoying the music—there’s this one club where the lighting is awful, actually—but yeah, the main thing is I can’t stop focusing on it, even though people keep telling me they don’t see anything. It’s starting to really get in the way of work and being social.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are significantly affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes. It’s not just annoying, it’s kind of taking over more than I want to admit.\n\nLike at work, I’ve missed ticket deadlines because I’ll get stuck before or after a video call thinking about how my face looks, and I’ve even joined calls late because I couldn’t get myself together with the camera. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… humiliating.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events if I knew people were going to be taking photos. Which is sad because I used to really look forward to those. I still love dancing—there’s this one song they play that always gets everyone up—but then I start thinking about pictures and angles and I just don’t go. So yeah, it’s definitely affecting my happiness.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social interactions or activities because you were worried about how others would perceive your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, especially if I know there might be photos. Salsa nights are the big one, because people are always taking pictures or posting little clips, and then I end up thinking about my face instead of just dancing.\n\nI’ve skipped some community events too for the same reason. It’s embarrassing because sometimes I’ll make up an excuse, like I’m tired or I have errands, but really it’s because I’m worried about how my face is going to look to people.\n\nAnd even at work, it’s affected me—like I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on camera, and I’ve missed ticket deadlines after getting stuck on it. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… awful. So yes, I guess I have been avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern always about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy, or do you sometimes worry about other aspects of your appearance as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that, yeah—the left side of my face looking uneven or puffy. That’s the thing I get stuck on, especially on video calls or if someone takes salsa photos.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll start thinking the whole photo looks bad, or the lighting makes me look strange—there’s this one place we dance where the lights are just terrible—but it always kind of comes back to that left side of my face. People keep telling me they don’t see it, which almost makes me feel more weird, because I still can’t stop noticing it. It’s really why I came in, honestly, because it’s starting to affect work and going out.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face always focused on the left side being uneven or puffy, or does it sometimes shift to other parts of your face?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the left side, honestly. Like the unevenness or puffiness there is what I keep coming back to, especially on video calls or in salsa photos.\n\nSometimes I’ll start fussing over the whole picture, like the angle or the lighting—there was this one photo from dancing where everyone else looked normal and I just kept zooming in, which is ridiculous because I know people were probably looking at the dancing, not my face. But even then, it kind of circles back to that left side.\n\nAnd people keep saying they don’t see anything, which should make me feel better, but it doesn’t really. That’s part of why I came in… I can’t seem to stop focusing on it, and it’s starting to mess with work and social stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't think about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe if I’m completely distracted for a bit, like if I’m troubleshooting something at work and someone’s printer is doing that thing where it says it’s offline even though it’s clearly not—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut no, it keeps coming back. That’s kind of why I came in. I can’t stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks, especially on video calls or in salsa photos. And everyone keeps telling me they don’t see anything, but that doesn’t really make it go away. It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff, so… yeah, I don’t feel like I get much of a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel like your concerns about your face are completely under control and don't interfere with what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, but… not really, not in the situations where it matters. Like if I’m just at home doing laundry or making coffee, maybe it’s in the background more. But at work, especially with video calls, it can get really out of hand.\n\nI’ve joined calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the camera preview—adjusting the angle, checking the lighting, turning it off and on. And then I’m late, and it looks unprofessional. I’ve also missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck on it instead of just finishing the support issue, which is embarrassing because normally I’m pretty reliable. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, not the face thing exactly, but the lateness and missed deadlines.\n\nAnd socially… salsa used to be such a fun thing for me. I mean, I still love the music and the community, and there’s this one song they always play that gets everyone on the floor—but if I know people are going to be taking photos, I’ll sometimes just not go. Same with community events. I’ll think, “Okay, there are going to be pictures posted later,” and then I talk myself out of it. So… no, I wouldn’t say it’s completely under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, indirectly at least. Like, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events when I knew people were going to be taking photos, and that means I’m not seeing people I normally would.\n\nIt’s not like my friends have done anything wrong. There’s this whole group chat after salsa sometimes where people send photos, and I just… I don’t know, I avoid it.\n\nAnd at work it’s been affecting things too—video calls, ticket deadlines. I’ve joined calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked, and I’ve missed deadlines because I got caught up in it. My supervisor brought that up in a performance review, which was awful. So I guess with friends and family it’s more that I pull back, and with work it’s more obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are making it hard for you to enjoy time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. And community events too, if I know there’ll probably be photos. Which is kind of sad, because those are people I actually like being around. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or I have errands, but really it’s because I don’t want to see myself in pictures later.\n\nAnd it’s not just friends… at work I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the preview screen, and I’ve missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck on it. My supervisor brought it up in a performance review, which was really embarrassing. So, yeah, it’s definitely getting in the way of enjoying things and just being present with people.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your concerns about your face are interfering with your ability to do your job or maintain relationships?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "patient_turn_index: 12\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

I’ve also skipped salsa nights and community events when I knew people would be taking photos.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Yeah… it is. I mean, I keep telling myself it’s not that bad because I can still do my job most days, but then I’ve missed ticket deadlines because I got stuck worrying about how I looked, and I’ve joined video calls late after messing with the camera preview and lighting.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess the main thing is my face. Specifically the left side—it looks uneven and kind of puffy to me, especially on video calls. I do computer support, so I’m on calls a lot, and I end up staring at my own little camera box instead of listening sometimes.\n\nAnd then there are salsa photos— I dance salsa, and people post pictures, and I’ll zoom in and compare sides of my face. Everyone keeps saying they don’t see what I’m talking about, which almost makes it worse because then I feel like I’m either crazy or they’re just being nice. It’s starting to affect work and social stuff, so… yeah, that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face being uneven and puffy only about how it looks, or are you also experiencing any other symptoms like pain or discomfort?\n\nPatient: It’s really about how it looks, I think. Like, I don’t have some big pain thing I came in for… it’s more that I can’t stop noticing the left side of my face looking uneven or puffy, especially on video calls.\n\nAnd then with salsa pictures—sorry, I know I keep coming back to that—but people post them and I end up zooming in, comparing my face, and everyone says they don’t see it. But I do, and then I can’t let it go. It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff because I’m so focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking at it, like when you're working or dancing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. Like even when I’m not looking at myself, it’s still kind of running in the background. At work I’ll be helping someone with their computer and then suddenly I’m thinking about how my face looked in the call window earlier, like the left side being puffy or uneven.\n\nAnd with salsa, even while I’m dancing I’ll think, “Oh no, if someone takes a photo from this angle it’s going to show.” Which is ridiculous because I’m supposed to be enjoying the music—there’s this one club where the lighting is awful, actually—but yeah, the main thing is I can’t stop focusing on it, even though people keep telling me they don’t see anything. It’s starting to really get in the way of work and being social.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are significantly affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes. It’s not just annoying, it’s kind of taking over more than I want to admit.\n\nLike at work, I’ve missed ticket deadlines because I’ll get stuck before or after a video call thinking about how my face looks, and I’ve even joined calls late because I couldn’t get myself together with the camera. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… humiliating.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events if I knew people were going to be taking photos. Which is sad because I used to really look forward to those. I still love dancing—there’s this one song they play that always gets everyone up—but then I start thinking about pictures and angles and I just don’t go. So yeah, it’s definitely affecting my happiness.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social interactions or activities because you were worried about how others would perceive your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, especially if I know there might be photos. Salsa nights are the big one, because people are always taking pictures or posting little clips, and then I end up thinking about my face instead of just dancing.\n\nI’ve skipped some community events too for the same reason. It’s embarrassing because sometimes I’ll make up an excuse, like I’m tired or I have errands, but really it’s because I’m worried about how my face is going to look to people.\n\nAnd even at work, it’s affected me—like I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on camera, and I’ve missed ticket deadlines after getting stuck on it. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… awful. So yes, I guess I have been avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern always about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy, or do you sometimes worry about other aspects of your appearance as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that, yeah—the left side of my face looking uneven or puffy. That’s the thing I get stuck on, especially on video calls or if someone takes salsa photos.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll start thinking the whole photo looks bad, or the lighting makes me look strange—there’s this one place we dance where the lights are just terrible—but it always kind of comes back to that left side of my face. People keep telling me they don’t see it, which almost makes me feel more weird, because I still can’t stop noticing it. It’s really why I came in, honestly, because it’s starting to affect work and going out.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face always focused on the left side being uneven or puffy, or does it sometimes shift to other parts of your face?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the left side, honestly. Like the unevenness or puffiness there is what I keep coming back to, especially on video calls or in salsa photos.\n\nSometimes I’ll start fussing over the whole picture, like the angle or the lighting—there was this one photo from dancing where everyone else looked normal and I just kept zooming in, which is ridiculous because I know people were probably looking at the dancing, not my face. But even then, it kind of circles back to that left side.\n\nAnd people keep saying they don’t see anything, which should make me feel better, but it doesn’t really. That’s part of why I came in… I can’t seem to stop focusing on it, and it’s starting to mess with work and social stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't think about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe if I’m completely distracted for a bit, like if I’m troubleshooting something at work and someone’s printer is doing that thing where it says it’s offline even though it’s clearly not—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut no, it keeps coming back. That’s kind of why I came in. I can’t stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks, especially on video calls or in salsa photos. And everyone keeps telling me they don’t see anything, but that doesn’t really make it go away. It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff, so… yeah, I don’t feel like I get much of a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel like your concerns about your face are completely under control and don't interfere with what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, but… not really, not in the situations where it matters. Like if I’m just at home doing laundry or making coffee, maybe it’s in the background more. But at work, especially with video calls, it can get really out of hand.\n\nI’ve joined calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the camera preview—adjusting the angle, checking the lighting, turning it off and on. And then I’m late, and it looks unprofessional. I’ve also missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck on it instead of just finishing the support issue, which is embarrassing because normally I’m pretty reliable. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, not the face thing exactly, but the lateness and missed deadlines.\n\nAnd socially… salsa used to be such a fun thing for me. I mean, I still love the music and the community, and there’s this one song they always play that gets everyone on the floor—but if I know people are going to be taking photos, I’ll sometimes just not go. Same with community events. I’ll think, “Okay, there are going to be pictures posted later,” and then I talk myself out of it. So… no, I wouldn’t say it’s completely under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, indirectly at least. It feels like it’s always there, honestly… or at least I’m always checking for it. The left side just looks uneven and kind of puffy to me, especially on video calls or in salsa photos.

Other people keep saying they don’t notice anything, which makes me feel a little ridiculous, but I still can’t stop focusing on it. Like I’ll be trying to answer something at work and then I’m looking at my own face in the corner of the screen. And salsa photos are worse because they’re just there afterward, and everyone’s sending them around like it’s no big deal.

So… I guess maybe it changes depending on lighting or angles, but in my head it’s pretty constant. It’s the main reason I came in, because it’s starting to mess with my work and my social life.

Like, I don’t have some big pain thing I came in for… it’s more that I can’t stop noticing the left side of my face looking uneven or puffy, especially on video calls.\n\nAnd then with salsa pictures—sorry, I know I keep coming back to that—but people post them and I end up zooming in, comparing my face, and everyone says they don’t see it. But I do, and then I can’t let it go. It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff because I’m so focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking at it, like when you're working or dancing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. Like even when I’m not looking at myself, it’s still kind of running in the background. At work I’ll be helping someone with their computer and then suddenly I’m thinking about how my face looked in the call window earlier, like the left side being puffy or uneven.\n\nAnd with salsa, even while I’m dancing I’ll think, “Oh no, if someone takes a photo from this angle it’s going to show.” Which is ridiculous because I’m supposed to be enjoying the music—there’s this one club where the lighting is awful, actually—but yeah, the main thing is I can’t stop focusing on it, even though people keep telling me they don’t see anything. It’s starting to really get in the way of work and being social.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are significantly affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes. It’s not just annoying, it’s kind of taking over more than I want to admit.\n\nLike at work, I’ve missed ticket deadlines because I’ll get stuck before or after a video call thinking about how my face looks, and I’ve even joined calls late because I couldn’t get myself together with the camera. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… humiliating.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events if I knew people were going to be taking photos. Which is sad because I used to really look forward to those. I still love dancing—there’s this one song they play that always gets everyone up—but then I start thinking about pictures and angles and I just don’t go. So yeah, it’s definitely affecting my happiness.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any social interactions or activities because you were worried about how others would perceive your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, especially if I know there might be photos. Salsa nights are the big one, because people are always taking pictures or posting little clips, and then I end up thinking about my face instead of just dancing.\n\nI’ve skipped some community events too for the same reason. It’s embarrassing because sometimes I’ll make up an excuse, like I’m tired or I have errands, but really it’s because I’m worried about how my face is going to look to people.\n\nAnd even at work, it’s affected me—like I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on camera, and I’ve missed ticket deadlines after getting stuck on it. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… awful. So yes, I guess I have been avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern always about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy, or do you sometimes worry about other aspects of your appearance as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that, yeah—the left side of my face looking uneven or puffy. That’s the thing I get stuck on, especially on video calls or if someone takes salsa photos.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll start thinking the whole photo looks bad, or the lighting makes me look strange—there’s this one place we dance where the lights are just terrible—but it always kind of comes back to that left side of my face. People keep telling me they don’t see it, which almost makes me feel more weird, because I still can’t stop noticing it. It’s really why I came in, honestly, because it’s starting to affect work and going out.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face always focused on the left side being uneven or puffy, or does it sometimes shift to other parts of your face?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the left side, honestly. Like the unevenness or puffiness there is what I keep coming back to, especially on video calls or in salsa photos.\n\nSometimes I’ll start fussing over the whole picture, like the angle or the lighting—there was this one photo from dancing where everyone else looked normal and I just kept zooming in, which is ridiculous because I know people were probably looking at the dancing, not my face. But even then, it kind of circles back to that left side.\n\nAnd people keep saying they don’t see anything, which should make me feel better, but it doesn’t really. That’s part of why I came in… I can’t seem to stop focusing on it, and it’s starting to mess with work and social stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't think about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe if I’m completely distracted for a bit, like if I’m troubleshooting something at work and someone’s printer is doing that thing where it says it’s offline even though it’s clearly not—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut no, it keeps coming back. That’s kind of why I came in. I can’t stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks, especially on video calls or in salsa photos. And everyone keeps telling me they don’t see anything, but that doesn’t really make it go away. It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff, so… yeah, I don’t feel like I get much of a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel like your concerns about your face are completely under control and don't interfere with what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, but… not really, not in the situations where it matters. Like if I’m just at home doing laundry or making coffee, maybe it’s in the background more. But at work, especially with video calls, it can get really out of hand.\n\nI’ve joined calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the camera preview—adjusting the angle, checking the lighting, turning it off and on. And then I’m late, and it looks unprofessional. I’ve also missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck on it instead of just finishing the support issue, which is embarrassing because normally I’m pretty reliable. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, not the face thing exactly, but the lateness and missed deadlines.\n\nAnd socially… salsa used to be such a fun thing for me. I mean, I still love the music and the community, and there’s this one song they always play that gets everyone on the floor—but if I know people are going to be taking photos, I’ll sometimes just not go. Same with community events. I’ll think, “Okay, there are going to be pictures posted later,” and then I talk myself out of it. So… no, I wouldn’t say it’s completely under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, indirectly at least. Like, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events when I knew people were going to be taking photos, and that means I’m not seeing people I normally would.\n\nIt’s not like my friends have done anything wrong. They’ll post pictures and everyone looks happy, and then I’ll get stuck thinking about how I’d look in them. There’s this whole group chat after salsa sometimes where people send photos, and I just… I don’t know, I avoid it.\n\nAnd at work it’s been affecting things too—video calls, ticket deadlines. I’ve joined calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked, and I’ve missed deadlines because I got caught up in it. My supervisor brought that up in a performance review, which was awful. So I guess with friends and family it’s more that I pull back, and with work it’s more obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are making it hard for you to enjoy time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean… I guess there’s a possibility, yeah. People keep telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about, or that it looks normal, and I try to believe that.

But then I’ll be on a video call and I see the little preview of myself, and the left side just looks so uneven and puffy to me that I can’t stop staring at it. Or after salsa, if someone posts photos, I zoom in and it’s like… there it is again. Even if nobody else notices.

That’s basically why I came in. I can’t seem to stop focusing on it, and it’s started affecting work and seeing people, so part of me knows maybe it’s less noticeable than I think, but it doesn’t feel that way when I’m looking at it.

But even then, it kind of circles back to that left side.\n\nAnd people keep saying they don’t see anything, which should make me feel better, but it doesn’t really. That’s part of why I came in… I can’t seem to stop focusing on it, and it’s starting to mess with work and social stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't think about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe if I’m completely distracted for a bit, like if I’m troubleshooting something at work and someone’s printer is doing that thing where it says it’s offline even though it’s clearly not—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut no, it keeps coming back. That’s kind of why I came in. I can’t stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks, especially on video calls or in salsa photos. And everyone keeps telling me they don’t see anything, but that doesn’t really make it go away. It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff, so… yeah, I don’t feel like I get much of a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel like your concerns about your face are completely under control and don't interfere with what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, but… not really, not in the situations where it matters. Like if I’m just at home doing laundry or making coffee, maybe it’s in the background more. But at work, especially with video calls, it can get really out of hand.\n\nI’ve joined calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the camera preview—adjusting the angle, checking the lighting, turning it off and on. And then I’m late, and it looks unprofessional. I’ve also missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck on it instead of just finishing the support issue, which is embarrassing because normally I’m pretty reliable. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, not the face thing exactly, but the lateness and missed deadlines.\n\nAnd socially… salsa used to be such a fun thing for me. I mean, I still love the music and the community, and there’s this one song they always play that gets everyone on the floor—but if I know people are going to be taking photos, I’ll sometimes just not go. Same with community events. I’ll think, “Okay, there are going to be pictures posted later,” and then I talk myself out of it. So… no, I wouldn’t say it’s completely under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, indirectly at least. Like, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events when I knew people were going to be taking photos, and that means I’m not seeing people I normally would.\n\nIt’s not like my friends have done anything wrong. They’ll post pictures and everyone looks happy, and then I’ll get stuck thinking about how I’d look in them. I mean, salsa used to be this thing where I could just go and have fun, and not think so much—well, mostly. There’s always someone taking pictures now, though. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or I have errands, but really it’s because I don’t want to see myself in pictures later.\n\nAnd it’s not just friends… at work I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the preview screen, and I’ve missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck on it. People keep telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about, or that it looks normal, and I try to believe that.\n\nBut then I’ll be on a video call and I see the little preview of myself, and the left side just looks so uneven and puffy to me that I can’t stop staring at it. Or after salsa, if someone posts photos, I zoom in and it’s like… there it is again. Even if nobody else notices.\n\nThat’s basically why I came in. I can’t seem to stop focusing on it, and it’s started affecting work and seeing people, so part of me knows maybe it’s less noticeable than I think, but it doesn’t feel that way when I’m looking at it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think it's possible that the left side of your face actually looks normal, but you just can't stop thinking it looks uneven or puffy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / computer_support_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can't stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks on video calls and in salsa photos, even though everyone keeps telling me they don't see anything, and it's starting to mess with my work and social life.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I know it’s possible. Like, logically, if everyone keeps telling me they don’t see anything, then maybe it does look normal and I’m just… stuck on it.

But then I see myself on a video call, or someone sends salsa photos, and I can’t not see the left side looking uneven or puffy. I’ll be trying to focus on work and suddenly I’m staring at my own face instead, which is ridiculous because there are actual tickets I’m supposed to be handling.

And with salsa, it’s like—well, people are just posting photos like it’s fun, which it is, or it used to be, and then I’m zooming in on my face instead of looking at the whole picture. So yes, maybe it’s normal, but I came in because I can’t stop focusing on it, and it’s starting to mess with work and seeing people.

I do computer support, so I’m on calls a lot, and I end up staring at my own little camera box instead of listening sometimes.\n\nAnd then there are salsa photos— I dance salsa, and people post pictures, and I’ll zoom in and compare sides of my face. Everyone keeps saying they don’t see what I’m talking about, which almost makes it worse because then I feel like I’m either crazy or they’re just being nice. It’s starting to affect work and social stuff, so… yeah, that’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face being uneven and puffy only about how it looks, or are you also experiencing any other symptoms like pain or discomfort?\n\nPatient: It’s really about how it looks, I think. Like, I don’t have some big pain thing I came in for… it’s more that I can’t stop noticing the left side of my face looking uneven or puffy, especially on video calls.\n\nAnd then with salsa pictures—sorry, I know I keep coming back to that—but people post them and I end up zooming in, comparing my face, and everyone says they don’t see it. But I do, and then I can’t let it go. It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff because I’m so focused on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the appearance of your face at times when you're not looking at it, like when you're working or dancing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. Like even when I’m not looking at myself, it’s still kind of running in the background. At work I’ll be helping someone with their computer and then suddenly I’m thinking about how my face looked in the call window earlier, like the left side being puffy or uneven.\n\nAnd with salsa, even while I’m dancing I’ll think, “Oh no, if someone takes a photo from this angle it’s going to show.” Which is ridiculous because I’m supposed to be enjoying the music—there’s this one club where the lighting is awful, actually—but yeah, the main thing is I can’t stop focusing on it, even though people keep telling me they don’t see anything. It’s starting to really get in the way of work and being social.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are significantly affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes. It’s not just annoying, it’s kind of taking over more than I want to admit.\n\nLike at work, I’ve missed ticket deadlines because I’ll get stuck before or after a video call thinking about how my face looks, and I’ve even joined calls late because I couldn’t get myself together with the camera. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… humiliating.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events if I knew people were going to be taking photos. Which is sad because I used to really look forward to those. I still love dancing—there’s this one song they play that always gets everyone up—but then I start thinking about pictures and angles and I just don’t go. It’s embarrassing because sometimes I’ll make up an excuse, like I’m tired or I have errands, but really it’s because I’m worried about how my face is going to look to people.\n\nAnd even at work, it’s affected me—like I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on camera, and I’ve missed ticket deadlines after getting stuck on it. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, which was… awful. So yes, I guess I have been avoiding things.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern always about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy, or do you sometimes worry about other aspects of your appearance as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that, yeah—the left side of my face looking uneven or puffy. Like the unevenness or puffiness there is what I keep coming back to, especially on video calls or in salsa photos.\n\nSometimes I’ll start fussing over the whole picture, like the angle or the lighting—there was this one photo from dancing where everyone else looked normal and I just kept zooming in, which is ridiculous because I know people were probably looking at the dancing, not my face. But even then, it kind of circles back to that left side.\n\nAnd people keep saying they don’t see anything, which should make me feel better, but it doesn’t really. That’s part of why I came in… I can’t seem to stop focusing on it, and it’s starting to mess with work and social stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't think about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy for a while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe if I’m completely distracted for a bit, like if I’m troubleshooting something at work and someone’s printer is doing that thing where it says it’s offline even though it’s clearly not—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nBut no, it keeps coming back. That’s kind of why I came in. I can’t stop focusing on how uneven and puffy the left side of my face looks, especially on video calls or in salsa photos. It’s starting to mess with work and social stuff, so… yeah, I don’t feel like I get much of a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations where you feel like your concerns about your face are completely under control and don't interfere with what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, but… not really, not in the situations where it matters. Like if I’m just at home doing laundry or making coffee, maybe it’s in the background more. But at work, especially with video calls, it can get really out of hand.\n\nI’ve joined calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the camera preview—adjusting the angle, checking the lighting, turning it off and on. And then I’m late, and it looks unprofessional. I’ve also missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck on it instead of just finishing the support issue, which is embarrassing because normally I’m pretty reliable. My supervisor actually brought it up in a performance review, not the face thing exactly, but the lateness and missed deadlines.\n\nAnd socially… salsa used to be such a fun thing for me. I mean, I still love the music and the community, and there’s this one song they always play that gets everyone on the floor—but if I know people are going to be taking photos, I’ll sometimes just not go. Same with community events. I’ll think, “Okay, there are going to be pictures posted later,” and then I talk myself out of it. So… no, I wouldn’t say it’s completely under control.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, indirectly at least. Like, I’ve skipped salsa nights and community events when I knew people were going to be taking photos, and that means I’m not seeing people I normally would.\n\nIt’s not like my friends have done anything wrong. They’ll post pictures and everyone looks happy, and then I’ll get stuck thinking about how I’d look in them. There’s this whole group chat after salsa sometimes where people send photos, and I just… I don’t know, I avoid it.\n\nAnd at work it’s been affecting things too—video calls, ticket deadlines. I’ve joined calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked, and I’ve missed deadlines because I got caught up in it. My supervisor brought that up in a performance review, which was awful. So I guess with friends and family it’s more that I pull back, and with work it’s more obvious.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are making it hard for you to enjoy time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, salsa used to be this thing where I could just go and have fun, and not think so much—well, mostly. There’s always someone taking pictures now, though. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or I have errands, but really it’s because I don’t want to see myself in pictures later.\n\nAnd it’s not just friends… at work I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the preview screen, and I’ve missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck on it. My supervisor brought it up in a performance review, which was really embarrassing. So, yeah, it’s definitely getting in the way of enjoying things and just being present with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are interfering with your ability to do your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I keep telling myself it’s not that bad because I can still do my job most days, but then I’ve missed ticket deadlines because I got stuck worrying about how I looked, and I’ve joined video calls late after messing with the camera preview and lighting.\n\nMy supervisor brought it up in a performance review, which was really embarrassing. And community events too, if I know there’ll probably be photos. Which is kind of sad, because those are people I actually like being around. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or I have errands, but really it’s because I don’t want to see myself in pictures later.\n\nAnd it’s not just friends… at work I’ve joined video calls late because I couldn’t get past how I looked on the preview screen, and I’ve missed some ticket deadlines because I got stuck on it. My supervisor brought it up in a performance review, which was really embarrassing. So, yeah, it’s definitely getting in the way of enjoying things and just being present with people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are interfering with your ability to do your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I keep telling myself it’s not that bad because I can still do my job most days, but then I’ve missed ticket deadlines because I got stuck worrying about how I looked, and I’ve joined video calls late after messing with the camera preview and lighting.\n\nMy supervisor brought it up in a performance review, which was really embarrassing. Not the face part, just the lateness and deadlines, but I knew why it was happening.\n\nAnd relationships, yeah, sort of… like with salsa nights, I’ve skipped them when I knew people would be taking photos. Same with community events. Which is frustrating because I actually like those people, and I used to look forward to going. But then I start thinking about photos getting posted, and I just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the left side of your face being uneven or puffy something that you think is always present, or do you think it changes at times?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s always there, honestly… or at least I’m always checking for it. The left side just looks uneven and kind of puffy to me, especially on video calls or in salsa photos.\n\nOther people keep saying they don’t notice anything, which makes me feel a little ridiculous, but I still can’t stop focusing on it. Like I’ll be trying to answer something at work and then I’m looking at my own face in the corner of the screen. And salsa photos are worse because they’re just there afterward, and everyone’s sending them around like it’s no big deal.\n\nSo… I guess maybe it changes depending on lighting or angles, but in my head it’s pretty constant. It’s the main reason I came in, because it’s starting to mess with my work and my social life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a possibility that the unevenness or puffiness on the left side of your face isn't actually there, or is less noticeable than you think?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess there’s a possibility, yeah. People keep telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about, or that it looks normal, and I try to believe that.\n\nBut then I’ll be on a video call and I see the little preview of myself, and the left side just looks so uneven and puffy to me that I can’t stop staring at it. Or after salsa, if someone posts photos, I zoom in and it’s like… there it is again. Even if nobody else notices.\n\nThat’s basically why I came in. I can’t seem to stop focusing on it, and it’s started affecting work and seeing people, so part of me knows maybe it’s less noticeable than I think, but it doesn’t feel that way when I’m looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that the left side of your face actually looks normal, but you just can't stop thinking it looks uneven or puffy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I know it’s possible. Like, logically, if everyone keeps telling me they don’t see anything, then maybe it does look normal and I’m just… stuck on it.\n\nBut then I see myself on a video call, or someone sends salsa photos, and I can’t not see the left side looking uneven or puffy. I’ll be trying to focus on work and suddenly I’m staring at my own face instead, which is ridiculous because there are actual tickets I’m supposed to be handling.\n\nAnd with salsa, it’s like—well, people are just posting photos like it’s fun, which it is, or it used to be, and then I’m zooming in on my face instead of looking at the whole picture. So yes, maybe it’s normal, but I came in because I can’t stop focusing on it, and it’s starting to mess with work and seeing people."
